     10.2   Communication Complexity

     In communication complexity, there are two parties, Alice and Bob, who
     wish to work together to compute a function of their inputs. Specifically,
     Alice has input x, Bob has input y, and their goal is to compute some
     function f (x, y) of their inputs, while exchanging as few bits as possible.
     Here, both Alice and Bob know the function f , but Bob does not know
     x and Alice does not know y. The cost of a communication protocol is
     the maximum number of bits exchanged over any pair of inputs, and the
     communication complexity of f is the least cost of any communication
     protocol computing f .
          As with query complexity, there are many variant models of inter-
     est. In the most basic setting, deterministic communication complexity
     (denoted Pcc ), Alice and Bob must always output f (x, y). In Á-error
     randomized communication complexity, Alice and Bob begin the proto-
     col by tossing a sequence r of random coins, after which they execute a
     deterministic communication protocol (which may depend on r), and
     they are only required to output the correct answer f (x, y) with proba-
     bility at least 1 ≠ Á over the choice of r. If Á = 1/3, the communication
     model is denoted BPPcc .
          In this survey, we will only consider private random coins, meaning
     that Alice and Bob both have access to their own random coins, but
     Bob can’t see Alice’s coins unless she sends them to him (which counts
     toward the communication cost) or vice versa.

     Randomized vs. deterministic communication, and the Equality func-
     tion. Randomized communication protocols can be far more efficient
     than deterministic ones for some functions. The prototypical example
     is the Equality function EQ, which takes as input two n-bit strings x
     and y, and evaluates to ≠1 if and only if x = y. It is known that the
     deterministic communication complexity of this problem is n (or n + 1
     if Alice and Bob are both required to know the output). But its Á-error
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     120            Approximate Rank Lower Bounds from Approximate Degree


     randomized communication complexity is just O(log(1/Á) + log n), and
     in particular its BPPcc complexity is just logarithmic in n.
          The idea is that the random string can be used to select a hash
     function h, and then Alice can send h(x) to Bob, who will output ≠1 if
     and only if h(x) = h(y). Suppose h is a random function with domain
     {≠1, 1}n and range {1, 2, . . . , 1/Á}. If x = y, then the probability (over
     the random choice of h) of this event is 1. On the other hand, if x ”= y,
     then the probability h(x) = h(y) is Á.4 In the private coin setting, the
     communication cost is what is required for Alice to send both h(x) and
     a description of the hash function h to Bob, as this lets Bob compute
     h(y) and compare it to h(x). Clearly, specifying h(x) requires just
     Álog2 (1/Á)Ë bits. Unfortunately, h does not have a short description if
     it is a random hash function. However, it turns out that h need not be
     a uniform random function; there are ways to choose h such that the
     above protocol works and Alice only needs to send O(log(1/Á) + log n)
     bits to Bob to specify h.

     Even more powerful communication models. The above example
     demonstrates that randomized communication protocols can be quite
     powerful, at least relative to deterministic ones. This means that we
     might expect proving lower bounds against randomized communication
     protocols to be difficult. Yet in this section, we are interested in proving
     lower bounds against even more powerful communication models. We
     are particularly interested in the following settings. First is quantum
     communication complexity (denoted BQPcc ). Roughly speaking, in a
     BQPcc protocol, Alice and Bob are allowed to exchange quantum bits
     rather than just classical bits, and must output f (x, y) with probability
     at least 2/3. As with BQPdt , the precise details of the BQPcc model
     will not be relevant to this survey.
         Second are the communication analogs of PPdt and UPPdt . PPcc (f )
     is the least d for which a randomized communication protocol in which
     Alice and Bob exchange at most d bits, and output f (x) with proba-
     bility at least 1/2 + 2≠d . UPPcc (f ) is defined identically, except the
     advantage over random guessing is only required to be strictly positive.
         4
           To see this, observe that since h is a random function with range R =
     {1, 2, . . . , 1/Á}, then h(x) and h(y) are random range elements. Hence, h(x) = h(y)
     with probability 1/|R| = 1/(1/‘) = ‘.
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     10.3. Query-to-Communication Lifting Theorems                                  121


     10.3    Lifting Theorems: Communication Lower Bounds from Query
             Lower Bounds

     A powerful approach to constructing hard communication problems is
     called “query-to-communication lifting”. In this approach, one starts
     with a function f : {≠1, 1}n æ {≠1, 1} that is hard in the correspond-
     ing query model, and transforms f into a communication problem
     by composing it with a simple communication problem g : {≠1, 1}m ◊
     {≠1, 1}m æ {≠1, 1} (g is typically called a “gadget” function). Specif-
     ically, in the communication problem F = f ¶ g, Alice and Bob
     are respectively given inputs x = (x1 , . . . , xn ) œ ({≠1, 1}m )n and
     y = (y1 , . . . , yn ) œ ({≠1, 1}m )n , and their goal is to compute F (x, y) =
     f (g(x1 , y1 ), . . . , g(xn , yn )). That is, Alice’s and Bob’s inputs define n
     independent copies of the communication problem g, and the outputs of
     these copies of g are fed into f to determine the function value F (x, y).
         The communication complexity of the lifted function F is always at
     most the query complexity of f times the deterministic communication
     complexity of g [38]. This is because Alice and Bob can “work together”
     to run a query algorithm A for f , and each time A queries an input i to f ,
     Alice and Bob can “answer” the query with g(xi , yi ). The communication
     cost of this protocol is the number of queries made by A, times the
     deterministic communication cost of g 5
         Lifting theorems show that for many communication models, if g is
     a “sufficiently complicated” gadget, then this is essentially the best that
     Alice and Bob can do. That is, the communication complexity of F is
     at least the query complexity of f . The term lifting refers to the fact
     that the theorem takes a weak lower bound (i.e., one that applies only
     in a query complexity setting) and lifts it up, or strengthens it, into a
     stronger lower bound (i.e., one that applies to a richer model, namely
     the corresponding communication model).
         In this section, we prove lifting theorems for PPcc and UPPcc . We
     will also show that an approximate degree lower bound for f “lifts” to
     a quantum communication lower bound for F . All of these results go
     through a matrix-analytic notion called approximate rank.

        5
          If the query algorithm for f is randomized, and the communication model is
     private coin, then Alice will also have to choose the random coins to use within the
     query algorithm, and send them to Bob.
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     122          Approximate Rank Lower Bounds from Approximate Degree


     Making sure the gadget is not “too simple”. If the gadget function
     g is too simple, then there will often be much better communication
     protocols for f ¶ g than the one described above in which Alice and Bob
     simulate a query protocol for f . That is, lifting theorems will fail to
     hold for f ¶ g.
         For example, if f = ün is the parity function on n bits, and g = ü2
     is the parity function on two bits, then f ¶ g = ün ¶ ü2 = ü2n is simply
     the parity function on 2n bits. There is a deterministic communication
     protocol for this function of constant cost: Alice sends Bob a single
     bit indicating whether the parity of x is even or odd. If the parity of
     x is even, then Bob outputs the parity of his input y; otherwise, Bob
     outputs the negation of the parity of y.
         In contrast, a consequence of the fact that deg± (ün ) = n is that
     any query protocol for f = ün requires (n) queries (this holds even
     for UPP query protocols, see Fact 10.2).
         Similar examples hold for other two-bit gadgets. For example, if
     f = ANDn and g = AND2 , then f ¶ g = AND2n . It is easily seen
     that there is a constant-cost deterministic communication protocol for
                                  Á ) = (Ôn) (Theorem 5.3), there is no
     AND2n . In contrast, since deg(f
     bounded-error query protocol protocol for f (not even a quantum one)
                           Ô
     of cost less than ( n) (see Theorem 10.1).
         Because of these examples, the gadget g appearing in the lifting
     theorems we establish in this section are necessarily defined over inputs
     consisting of more than 2 bits, but the input size to g is still constant
     (specifically, our g takes six bits as input).

     10.4   Communication Lower Bounds via Approximate Rank

     Given a matrix M œ {≠1, 1}m◊n , the Á-approximate rank of M , denoted
     rank
      Á Á (M ) is the smallest r such that there exists a rank-r matrix R
     satisfying |Mi,j ≠ Ri,j | Æ Á for all (i, j) œ [m] ◊ [n]. Conceptually, Á-
     approximate rank can be thought of as a matrix-analytic analog of
     approximate degree: It is asking for the “lowest-complexity” matrix
     (as measured by rank) that approximates M entry-wise up to error Á,
     just as the Á-approximate degree of f asks for the “lowest-complexity
     polynomials” (as measured by degree) that approximates f point-wise
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     10.4. Communication Lower Bounds via Approximate Rank                            123


     up to error Á.6 As with approximate degree, if Á is not specified, its
     value is interpreted as 1/3 by convention.
         Similarly, the sign-rank of M , denoted rank± (M ) is the least rank
     r of a matrix R that agrees in sign with M entry-wise. Just as the
     threshold degree of f equals the limit of its Á-approximate degree as
     Á approaches 1 from below, so too does the sign-rank of M equal the
     limit of its approximate rank as Á approaches 1 from below.

     Communication problems as matrices. Any communication problem
     f (x, y) can be viewed as a matrix. Specifically, if x œ {≠1, 1}n and
     y œ {≠1, 1}m , consider the 2n ◊ 2m matrix Mf whose (x, y)’th entry is
     f (x, y) (here, we are using x to index the rows of M and y to index the
     columns).

     Communication lower bounds via matrix analysis. It turns out that
     the complexity of f (x, y) in various communication models is closely
     related to the matrix-analytic notion of approximate rank. To illustrate
     why variants of matrix rank and communication complexity are often
     related, the following fact spells out the details of such a relationship
     between deterministic communication complexity and exact matrix
     rank.

     Fact 10.4. The deterministic communication complexity of f : {≠1, 1}n
     ◊{≠1, 1}n æ {≠1, 1} is lower bounded by the logarithm of the rank
     (over the real numbers) of the communication matrix Mf .

     Proof. In communication complexity, the term rectangle refers to any
     subset of the inputs with a product structure, i.e. any set of the form
     A ◊ B for A, B ™ {≠1, 1}n .
        A basic fact regarding deterministic communication protocols is
     the so-called rectangle property. This states that any protocol with

        6
          A more apt analogy is that Á-approximate rank is a matrix analog of Á-
     approximate sparsity, meaning the least number of monomials in an Á-approximation
     for the target function. This is because a rank-1 matrix can be thought of as a
     matrix-analog of a monomial: just as an s-sparse polynomial can be written as
     a linear combination of s monomials, a rank-r matrix can be written as a linear
     combination of r rank-1 matrices. This analogy is described in detail in Section 10.4.
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107

     communication cost c for a function f partitions the input set {≠1, 1}n ◊
     {≠1, 1}n into at most 2c rectangles R1 , . . . , R2c such that f is constant
     on each rectangle in the partition. That is, if (x, y) and (xÕ , y Õ ) lie in the
     same rectangle Ri then f (x, y) = f (xÕ , y Õ ). Each rectangle Ri is referred
     to as monochromatic for f .
         To see why the rectangle property holds, let us refer to a record
     of all bits exchanged by Alice and Bob during the execution of the
     communication protocol as a transcript. Say that an input (x, y) to f is
     consistent with a transcript if Alice and Bob produce that transcript
     when they run the communication protocol on (x, y). It is easily shown
     by induction on the number of rounds of the communication protocol
     that if (x, y) and (xÕ , y Õ ) are both consistent with a given transcript,
     then so is (x, y Õ ) and (xÕ , y). That is, the set of inputs consistent with
     any particular transcript form a rectangle. Intuitively, this means that
     if Alice has input x or xÕ , then the protocol does not permit her to
     tell the difference between Bob holding input y vs. holding input y Õ .
     And similarly Bob cannot tell the difference between Alice holding x vs.
     holding xÕ . This is compatible with the protocol computing the function
     f only if f (x, y) = f (x, y Õ ) = f (xÕ , y Õ ) = f (xÕ , y Õ ).
         The rectangle property then follows from the observation that there
     are at most 2c distinct transcripts that can be generated by a commu-
     nication protocol of cost c.
         With the rectangle property in hand, Fact 10.4 is derived via the
     following analysis. For a rectangle R = A ◊ B, define the matrix MR
     via:                          Y

                       (MR )x,y =
                                   [0 otherwise.

          It is easy to see that MR has rank 1, because its row space is spanned
     by the indicator vector of B (and similarly its column space is spanned
     by the indicator vector of A). That is, every row x of MR is either all-0s
     (if x is not in A) or equal to the indicator vector of B (if x is in A).
     Similarly, every column of MR is either the all-0s vector, or equal to
     the indicator vector of A.
          If f has deterministic communication complexity at most c, let
     R1 , . . . , R2c be the partition of {≠1, 1}n ◊ {≠1, 1}n into at most 2c

     above. Then Mf = 2i=1 MRi expresses Mf as a sum of at most 2c
     matrices of rank one. By sub-additivity of matrix rank, this implies
     that the rank of Mf is at most 2c .

         Fact 10.4 is a communication analog of the fact (see Section 10.1)
     that Fourier degree lower bounds deterministic query complexity. In

     Fact 10.2, and Fact 10.3. Specifically, just as these earlier results show

          With Facts 10.5-10.7 in hand, we turn to the following task. Given
     a function f : {≠1, 1}n æ {≠1, 1} of Á-approximate degree at least d,
     identify a communication problem F := f ¶ g for a function g(x, y)
     on O(1) bits such that rank
                               Á Á (MF ) is at least 2d . In this manner, we
     “lift” approximate degree lower bounds for f (which on their own suf-
     fice to establish query lower bounds) to BQPcc , UPPcc , and PPcc
     communication lower bounds for F .

     Approximate rank lower bounds for composed functions. In Sec-
     tion 7.2, we used dual block composition to establish hardness-amplifi-
     cation results via block-composition. That is, we showed that f ¶ g is
     harder to approximate by low-degree polynomials than f or g alone.
     In this section we will use dual block composition to show that f ¶ g
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107

     is harder in a different sense: whereas f is hard for query algorithms
     and Á-approximation via low-degree polynomials, f ¶ g will be hard for
     communication protocols and Á-approximation via low-rank matrices.
     Theorem 10.8. Let f : {≠1, 1}n to {≠1, 1} satisfy degÁ (f ) Ø d. There

         We will prove this result in two steps.
        • First, we will show that composing f with a certain 3-bit gadget
          idx : {≠1, 1}3 æ {≠1, 1}, called the indexing gadget, yields a func-
          tion f ¶ idx with large Á-approximate weight, specifically weight at
          least 2d . By approximate weight, we mean the minimum-weight
          polynomial that Á-approximates f , where the weight of a poly-
          nomial is the sum of its coefficients over the parity basis (see
          Section 2.1).

        • Second, we will show that composing f ¶ idx with the two-bit XOR
          gadget, ü2 , yields a function F with the claimed approximate
          rank lower bound.
         Hence, the 6-bit gadget g in Theorem 10.8 is simply the block-
     composition 3-bit indexing gadget and the two-bit XOR gadget. The-
     orem 10.8 is highly similar to [107, Theorem 8.1] in Sherstov’s paper
     introducing the so-called pattern matrix method, and indeed many of
     the calculations and notions that we use to prove Theorem 10.8 closely
     follow Sherstov’s analysis. Still, we believe that our two-step presenta-
     tion to proving Theorem 10.8 is subtantially simpler and more intuitive
     than prior treatments in the literature [81], [86], [107], [123].8
         Both Step 1 and Step 2 above can be understood as proving optimal-
     ity of simple and natural techniques for Á-approximating h := f ¶ idx by
         8
          A minor downside of our two-step approach is that we obtain a 6-bit gadget g,
     which is slightly larger than the 4-bit gadget in Sherstov’s pattern-matrix method
     [107]. Still, any constant-sized gadget suffices for all applications we cover in this
     survey. Other works have attempted to characterize the class of gadgets g for which
     Theorem 10.8 holds [82], which is not a focus in this survey.

     Step 1: From high approximate degree to high approximate weight.

     The optimal approximation technique. Theorem 10.9 shows that the
     following approach to constructing a low-weight Á-approximation to
     f ¶ idx is essentially optimal: take the lowest degree Á-approximation p
     to f , and let q be the multilinear polynomial that exactly computes idx,
     and consider the polynomial obtained by composing p and q. Clearly,
     p ¶ q Á-approximates f ¶ idx, and since idx is a Boolean function on
     only 3 bits, weight(q) Æ 3. Thus, weight(p ¶ q) Æ weight(p) · 3deg(p) . If
     weight(p) Æ 2O(d) , Theorem 10.9 proves a matching lower bound up to
     a constant factor in the exponent, and with an additive 2≠d/2 loss in
     the error for which the lower bound holds.
         Before proving Theorem 10.9, we introduce a dual formulation

     Discussion. It is worth reflecting upon what properties of the 3-bit
     indexing gadget idx we exploited in the proof of Theorem 10.9, to ensure
     that f ¶ idx had large Á-approximate weight. We use just two properties
     of idx(xi , yi , zi ) = 12 (xi ≠ xi zi + yi + yi zi ). First, that it is balanced, i.e.,
     its degree-0 Fourier coefficient is 0, and second, that all of its Fourier
     coefficients have magnitude at most 1/2.
          The balanced property ensured that idx (suitably scaled to have
     ¸1 -norm 1) is self-dual, in the sense of witnessing that its own threshold
     degree is at least 1. This meant that the dual block composition Â ı „ of
     Â with „ = 18 idx is just a scaled version of the literal block composition
     Â ¶ „. This in turn enabled us to compute the Fourier coefficients
     of Â ı „. We exploited the second property (that all of idx’s Fourier
     coefficients are 1/2) to conclude that the Fourier coefficients of Â ı „
     itself are exponentially small as required for any approximate-weight
     dual witness.

     Some simpler gadgets and why they fail. There are two natural
     gadgets that are even simpler than idx, namely the two-bit ü and AND
     gadgets. As discussed at the end of Section 10.3, our lifting theorems

     In the latter case,

     Step 2: From high approximate weight to high approximate rank.
     Let h : {≠1, 1}m æ {≠1, 1} be any Boolean function and define

     is to show that if h has large approximate weight, then MF has large
     approximate rank.

     The optimal approximation technique. Theorem 10.9 shows that the
     following approach to constructing a low-rank Á-approximation to the
     matrix MF is essentially optimal: take the sparsest polynomial p (over
     the parity basis) that Á-approximates h, let P (x, y) = p(x ü y), and
     approximate MF with the matrix MP . Since p is an Á-approximation
     to h, clearly, |(MF )x,y ≠ (MP )x,y | Æ Á for all x, y œ {≠1, 1}m .
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     10.4. Communication Lower Bounds via Approximate Rank                 133


         Moreover, rank(MP ) is at most sparsity(p) (recall from Section 2.1
     that the sparsity of p is the number of non-zero Fourier coefficients of
     p). To see this, for any subset S ™ [m], let us abuse notation and view
     ‰S as a column vector of length 2m whose y’th entry is ‰S (y). Then
     clearly ‰S · ‰TS is a rank-1 matrix with 2m rows and 2m columns, and
     with (x, y)’th entry equal to ‰S (x) · ‰S (y) = ‰S (x ü y). Hence, we can
     write                         ÿ           1         2
                             MP =       p̂(S) · ‰S · ‰TS
                                  S™[n]

     as a sum of at most sparsity(p)-many rank-1 matrices. Since matrix
     rank is subadditive, we conclude that the rank of MP is at most the
     Fourier sparsity of p.

     Discussion and intuition. In this section, we show that this technique
     for approximating F (x, y) = h ¶ ü2 by a low-rank matrix function P is
     essentially optimal. That is, we show that for MF to be ÁÕ -approximated
     by a rank-r matrix, h must be Á-approximated by a sparse polynomial
     p for some Á that is very close to ÁÕ . Here, the sparsity of p is roughly
     proportional to r, the rank of the approximation to MF .
         To be more precise, our analysis directly shows that if MF is ÁÕ -
     approximated by a rank-r matrix where r ¥ 2d , then h can be ÁÕ +2≠ (d)
     approximated by a polynomial q whose Fourier weight weight(q) is not
     much larger than r. In turn, any weight-W polynomial q can be ÁÕÕ -
     approximated by a polynomial p of sparsity at most ¸ := 100·W m/(ÁÕÕ )2 .
     This follows from a probabilistic construction due to Bruck and Smolen-
     sky [36]: randomly sample ¸ Fourier basis functions ‰S1 , . . . , ‰S¸ with
     replacement, where ‰S is chosen with probability proportional to |q̂(S)|,
                       q
     and let p(x) = 1¸ ¸i=1 ‰Si . Chernoff bounds imply that, for each input
     x œ {≠1, 1}m , with probability at least 1 ≠ 2≠2m , |p(x) ≠ q(x)| Æ ÁÕÕ .
     Hence, a union bound over all x œ {≠1, 1}m guarantees that with non-
     zero probability, |p(x) ≠ q(x)| Æ ÁÕÕ for all x œ {≠1, 1}m . Putting every-
     thing together, we conclude as claimed that if MF has (ÁÕ )-approximate
     rank at most r ¥ 2d , then h can be (Á + 2≠ (d) )-approximated by a
     polynomial of sparsity 2 (d) .
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107

     Overview of the proof. Recall that our goal is to show that if F (x, y) =
     h(x ü y) and h has large approximate weight, then MF has large
     approximate rank. Let ‹ : {≠1, 1}m æ R be the dual witness for the
     fact that h has large approximate weight, as per Section 10.4. Then
     ‹ is well-correlated with h but has tiny Fourier coefficients, meaning
     it is poorly correlated with any parity function ‰S (regardless of the
     degree of ‰S ). Letting ÷(x, y) = ‹(x ü y), this means that ÷ is poorly
     correlated with the rank-1 matrix ‰S · ‰TS .
          The rest of the analysis essentially shows that ÷ is effectively a dual
     witness for the high approximate rank of MF . This proceeds as follows.
          The first thing we do is show that M‹ has small spectral norm
     (i.e., all of its eigenvalues have small magnitude). We show this by
     establishing that the eigenvalues of M‹ are exactly given by the Fourier
     coefficients of ‹ (up to scaling), with the corresponding eigenspaces
     precisely given by the rank-1 matrices ‰S · ‰TS . Intuitively, this result
     means that not only is M‹ poorly correlated with the “special” rank-1
     matrices of the form ‰S · ‰TS , but in fact M‹ is poorly correlated with
     all rank-1 matrices.
          With the above spectral norm bound in hand, the remainder of
     the analysis mimics the following first-principles proof that a dual
     polynomial Â : {≠1, 1}n æ R indeed lower bounds the Á-approximate
     degree of f .

     First-principles proof that a dual polynomial lower bounds approxi-
     mate degree. If Â has pure high degree d, ¸1 -norm 1, and

                              xœ{≠1,1}n

     then Â rules out a degree-d Á-approximation p for f via the following
     calculation. On the one hand, since p has degree at most d and Â has
     pure high degree d, we know that
                                       ÿ
                          ÈÂ, pÍ =               Â(x)p(x) = 0.            (10.9)
                                     xœ{≠1,1}n
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     10.4. Communication Lower Bounds via Approximate Rank                        135


        On the other hand, since p approximates f and Â is strictly greater
     than Á-correlated with f ,
                      ÿ                         ÿ

     This contradicts Equation (10.9).
         Our proof in this section ports this argument to the matrix setting,
     with the matrix MF in place of f and ÷ in place of the dual polynomial
     Â for f . The spectral norm bound of Equation (10.17) for M÷ will mimic
     the effect of Â being uncorrelated with low-degree polynomials, as it
     implies that M÷ is only very weakly correlated with low-rank matrices.
     Meanwhile, since ‹ is well-correlated with h, we can show that M÷ is
     well-correlated with MF (our proof of this exploits the fact that ÷ can be
     expressed as the dual block composition of ‹ with a dual polynomial for
     the fact that deg± (ü2 ) Ø 2). Together, these two facts imply that MF
     cannot be approximated well by low-rank matrices, via a calculation
     analogous to Equation (10.10).
         The formal analysis follows.

     Bounding the spectral norm of M÷ . Recall that the spectral norm
     of a symmetric matrix M (Definition 9.1) is its largest eigenvalue in
     absolute value. The following lemma bounds the spectral norm of M÷ in
     terms of the Fourier spectrum of ‹. This lemma is folklore; we present
     a proof given in Mande’s thesis [88, Lemma 2.2.3].

     Lemma 10.11. Let ‹ : {≠1, 1}m æ R be a real-valued function, let

                    Á       n ) Ø ( n), Theorem 10.12 implies that

     BQPcc (NORn ¶ g) Ø ( n) where g is the 6-bit gadget from Theo-
     rem 10.8. We now explain that NOR¶g can be embedded into an instance
     of DISJ on O(n) bits. That is, Alice and Bob can reduce the task of
     computing (NOR ¶ g) (x, y) to the task of evaluating DISJN (xÕ , y Õ ) where
     N = O(n), xÕ depends only on x, and y Õ depends     Ô only on y. Hence,
     any communication protocol for DISJN of cost o( N ) would imply a
                                                      Ô
     communication protocol for NOR ¶ g of cost o( n).
         First, observe that for any gadget function g defined on a constant
     number of bits, OR ¶ g is computed by a DNF formula of size O(n).
     This is because any function g on O(1) bits is computable by a DNF of
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     10.4. Communication Lower Bounds via Approximate Rank                  141


     size O(1). Hence, OR ¶ g is computed by a depth-three circuit of size
     O(n), where the top two layers are OR gates. By collapsing the adjacent
     layers of OR gates into a single OR gate, we obtain a DNF of size O(n).
          Next, we explain that for any DNF F (x, y) of size N , computing
     F (x, y) can be reduced to an instance of DISJN (xÕ , y Õ ) where xÕ depends
     only x and y Õ depends only on y. Write F (x, y) = OR(C1 (x, y), . . . ,
     CN (x, y)) where each Ci is a conjunction (i.e., an AND of literals). We
     can partition the inputs to Ci into two halves, say, A and B: those
     literals fed into Ci that depend on x, and those that depend on y. Define
     xÕi = ≠1 if and only all literals in A evaluate to true, and define yiÕ = ≠1
     if and only if all literals in B evaluate to true. Then F (x, y) = 1 if and
     only if DISJ(xÕ , y Õ ) = ≠1.
          Hence, Alice and Bob can determine whether (NORn ¶ g)(x, y) = 1
     by representing NOR ¶ g as a DNF of size N = O(n), transforming the
     DNF into an equivalent instance (xÕ , y Õ ) of DISJN as per the previous
     paragraph, applying a BQPcc protocol for DISJN on input (xÕ , y Õ ), and
     negating the output. If the BQPcc protocol for DISJN has communica-
     tion cost c, then so does the resulting protocol for NORn ¶ g.

     Lower bounds for other communication classes. In characterizing
     the PPcc complexity of f ¶ g in terms of approximate degree of f ,
     Fact 10.3 has applications to additional communication complexity
     classes, notably QMAcc , where QMA is the quantum analog of NP.
     This is because it is known that any QMAcc protocol can be simulated
     by a PPcc protocol with at most a quadratic blowup in cost [90], [130].
     Hence, any PPcc lower bound for a function also implies a QMAcc
     lower bound. For example, combined with Theorem 10.15, this yields
           Ô
     an ( n) QMAcc lower bound for the inner product function, which is
     tight up to a logarithmic factor by a well-known result of Aaronson and
     Wigderson [7]. Other works have used approximate degree to prove tight
     or nearly tight QMA lower bounds for the Disjointness function [74], the
     permutation testing problem [120], and a problem called approximate
     counting [5].

     MAJ ¶ LTF circuit lower bounds.
     Recall that the Majority function MAJ, takes as input a bit-vector
     in {≠1, 1}n and outputs ≠1 if and only if at least n/2 of the bits
     are ≠1. Meanwhile, a linear threshold function (LTF) is any Boolean
     function f of threshold degree at most 1. That is, there are real weights
                                                                  q
     w0 , . . . , wn œ R such that f (x1 , . . . , xn ) = sgn(w0 + ni=1 wi xi ). An LTF
     is also called a halfspace.
          A MAJ ¶ LTF circuit is any depth-two circuit of size s + 1 that
     outputs the majority vote of s LTFs. In this section, we will see that a
     PPcc lower bound for a function F implies a lower bound on the size
     of MAJ ¶ LTF circuits computing F .
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     144          Approximate Rank Lower Bounds from Approximate Degree


         Here are two reasons to care about such lower bounds. First, a
     notorious open problem in circuit complexity is to identify an explicit
     Boolean function f that cannot be computed by LTF ¶ LTF circuits of
     polynomial size. MAJ ¶ LTF is a natural subclass of such circuits, so
     proving lower bounds against such circuits is a natural step toward
     resolving the LTF ¶ LTF question. Second, as discussed in Section 5.3,
     Allender [8] showed that quasipolynomial-size depth-3 majority circuits
     can compute all of AC0 , and for a long time it was open whether the
     same is true of depth-2 majority circuits. The lower bounds in this
     section show that this is not the case, even for MAJ ¶ LTF circuits.
     Theorem 10.18 ([93]). Suppose the function F (x, y) is computable by
     a MAJ ¶ LTF circuit C of size s + 1 Ø n. Then PPcc (F ) Æ O(log2 s).
         Combining Theorem 10.18 with Theorem 10.16 yields an AC0 func-
     tion that cannot be computed by MAJ ¶ LTF circuits of size smaller
              1/3
     than 2 (n ) .

     Proof. Assume for simplicity that s is odd. In the PPcc protocol for
     F , Alice uses her private randomness to select a random LTF gate of
     C and send the identity of this gate to Bob. This costs log s bits of
     communication. Letting Á = 1/s2 , Alice and Bob then execute an Á-
     error randomized communication protocol (described below) to evaluate
     the LTF gate at (x, y) and output the result. As we will show, they
     can accomplish this with communication just O(log2 s). The success
     probability of this protocol is at least 1/2 + 1/(2s) ≠ 1/s2 > 1/2 + 1/(3s).
     This is because on input (x, y), at least (s + 1)/2 out of the s LTF gates
     in C output F (x, y). Accordingly, the PPcc cost of this communication
     protocol is O(log2 s).
         Let x, y œ {≠1, 1}n and let

                                         i=1             j=1

     be any LTF; here is the randomized communication protocol for eval-

         Clearly, to compute G(x, y), it is enough to determine whether
                                              Q                 R

                               ÿ                   n

     Let X and Y denote the left hand and right hand size of Equation
     (10.22) respectively, and observe that X and Y are integers of magnitude
     nO(n) , and hence can be represented in binary with ¸ Æ O(n log n) bits.
     Let X ú , Y ú œ {≠1, 1}¸ denote these binary representations. Since X ú is
     independent of y and Y ú is independent of x, Alice knows X ú and Bob
     knows Y ú .
         We saw in Section 10.2 that there is an Á-error private-coin random-
     ized communication protocol for the Equality function on n bits with
     cost O(log(n) + log(1/Á)). First, Alice and Bob can run the Equality
     protocol on input (X ú , Y ú ) to determine whether X ú = Y ú . If so, they
     know whether or not Equation (10.22) holds. If not, the idea is to set
     Á Æ 1/(s2 · n) and to use this subroutine to perform a binary search to
     determine the highest-order bit at which X ú and Y ú disagree. This is
     sufficient information for Bob to determine whether or not Equation
     (10.22) holds, and hence to determine G(x, y).
         That is, first Alice and Bob run the Equality protocol on input the
     first half of X ú and Y ú to determine if X ú and Y ú agree on their first
     ¸/2 bits; if no, they recurse on the first half of X ú and Y ú ; if yes, they
     recurse on the second half. They continue this process until they have
     found a bit i such that Xiú ”= Yiú , yet X ú and Y ú agree on their first
     i ≠ 1 bits.
         The total number of invocations of the Equality protocol during
     this binary search procedure is at most O(log ¸) = O(log n). Since
     each invocation of the Equality protocol errs with probability at most
     1/(s2 · n), the probability that any of the invocations fail is at most
     O(log n/(s2 n)). Hence Alice and Bob successfully output G(x, y) with
     probability at least 1 ≠ o(1/s2 ) as desired. The total communication cost
     of the protocol is O(log(¸)·log(1/Á)) = O(log(n)·log(s2 ·n)) = O(log2 s),
     where the final equality holds because we assumed s + 1 Ø n.
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     146          Approximate Rank Lower Bounds from Approximate Degree


     10.5   Sign-Rank Lower Bounds

     Unfortunately, the additive loss of 2≠O(d) in the error parameter of
     Theorem 10.8 is devastating if our goal is to obtain sign-rank lower
     bounds on f ¶ g. This is because a sign-rank lower bound requires an
     Á-approximate rank lower bound for any Á < 1, including values of Á
     that might be (significantly) closer to 1 than 1 ≠ 2≠O(d) . Yet the loss of
     2≠ (d) in the error parameter prevents proving an Á-approximate rank
     lower bound for Á > 1 ≠ 2≠ (d) .
        Here is our dream theorem in this context, which unfortunately
     remains an unproven conjecture:

     Conjecture 10.19. Let g be the six-bit gadget from Theorem 10.8, and
     let f : {≠1, 1}n æ {≠1, 1} be any Boolean function with deg± (f ) Ø d.
     Then rank± (f ¶ g) Ø 2d .

         Unfortunately, we only know how to prove Conjecture 10.19 if
     deg± (f ) Ø d is witnessed by a dual polynomial Â satisfying an addi-
     tional smoothness condition. This condition requires that Â is “reason-
     ably large” on all inputs in {≠1, 1}n . Specifically, |Â(x)| needs to be
     at least 2≠d/2 · 2≠n for all x œ {≠1, 1}n . Fortunately, as we will see
     later (Section 10.5), threshold degree lower bounds for many important
     functions can be proven via such smooth dual witnesses.

     Theorem 10.20. Let g be the six-bit gadget from Theorem 10.8, and
     let f : {≠1, 1}n æ {≠1, 1} be any Boolean function with deg± (f ) Ø d
     and such that there is a dual witness Â for this fact (see Section 6)
     satisfying |Â(x)| Ø 2≠d/2 · 2≠n for all x œ {≠1, 1}n . Let F = f ¶ g. Then
     rank± (MF ) Ø 2d/3 .

     Overview of the proof. Let p : {≠1, 1}n æ R be a polynomial that
     agrees in sign with f and assume that |p(x)| Æ 1 for all x œ {≠1, 1}n .
     Then minxœ{≠1,1}n |p(x)| is called the margin of f : it is essentially the
     “closest” p gets to disagreeing in sign with f at any input.
         There are known Boolean functions that have threshold degree d
     but any degree-d polynomial agreeing in sign with f has margin 2≠n ,
                                                                              d


     which is doubly-exponentially small (in d) [48], [98], [99]. A key lemma of
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107

     Forster [59] shows that, nonetheless, any function f of threshold degree
     at most d is sign-represented by a polynomial p with large average
     margin (by large, we mean “only” singly-exponentially small in d, rather
     than doubly). That is, if deg± (f ) Æ d, then11 there exists a degree d
     polynomial p such that

         As we now explain, a threshold degree dual witness Â (see Section

     limited way. Specifically, suppose p has degree at most d, |p(x)| Æ 1 for
     all x œ {≠1, 1}n , p satisfies Equation (10.24) and, in place of Equation
     (10.23), we have:
      (bounded sign errors) |p(x) ≠ f (x)| Æ 1 + 2≠3d for all x œ {≠1, 1}n .

         Here, Equation (10.27) follows from the fact that sgn(Â(x))·f (x) > 0

         To prove Theorem 10.20, we essentially perform the above analysis
     in the matrix setting, whereby f is replaced by F = f ¶ g, and Â is
     replaced with ÷ (the “approximate-rank dual witness” from the proof
     of Theorem 10.8).

     Why this analysis overcomes the 2≠ (d) loss in the error parameter
     of Theorem 10.8. Intuitively, if the dual witness for f is smooth,
     then the analysis above establishes a lower bound on the degree of Á-
     approximating polynomials p even when Á can be as large as 1 + 2≠ (d)
     (see Equation (10.25)), so long as p has average margin at least 2≠d .
     And the assumption that p has this large an average margin is without
     loss of generality by Forster’s lemma.
         Put another way, the analysis above does not actually avoid the
     2≠ (d) loss in the error parameter Á incurred in Theorem 10.8, but it
     does render the loss innocuous. Because the smooth dual witness Â
     lower bounds the degree of polynomials even if they make errors as
     large as Á = 1 + 2≠ (d) (so long as the average margin is large), a 2≠ (d)
     degradation in the error parameter does not prevent the “degraded”
     error from being arbitrarily close to 1.
         Digging deeper, we can pinpoint the reason that Theorem 10.8
     experiences an additive loss of 2≠ (d) in the error parameter, and
     see why our analysis here circumvents the issue if Â is smooth. A
     threshold degree dual witness Â for f rules out the existence of a degree-
     d sign-representation of f because Â is uncorrelated with all degree d
     polynomials, but by virtue of agreeing in sign with f at all inputs, Â is
     positively correlated with any sign-representation p of f . The reason
     for the additive 2≠d loss in the error parameter of Theorem 10.8 is
     that, when porting this style of analysis to the matrix-analytic setting,
     we cannot show that the “matrix dual witness” ÷ = Â ı „ ı µ has
     zero correlation with rank-r matrices. Rather, we can only show that
     ÷ has very low correlation with rank-r matrices (correlation roughly
                        Ô
     bounded by 2≠d / r). Fortunately, the smoothness condition on the
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     10.5. Sign-Rank Lower Bounds                                           149


     dual witness Â turns out to guarantee that ÷’s correlation with any
     sign-representation of F with large average margin is not only positive,
     but is in fact noticeably so (roughly, at least 2≠d/2 times the average
     margin, which in turn is at least 1/r by Forster’s lemma). This is enough
     to conclude that any sign-representation for F must have large rank (at
     least 2 (d) ).

     Forster and was explicitly distilled by Razborov and Sherstov [102]. We
     omit the proof from this survey, directing the interested reader to [102,
     Appendix B].

     Communication applications.
     Since the parity function f = ün on n has threshold degree n, and this
     is witnessed by the dual polynomial 2≠n · ün , which is perfectly smooth,
     we obtain an explicit communication problem f ¶ g with linear UPPcc
     complexity.

     Corollary 10.22. Let f = ün and F = f ¶ g be the composition of f
     with the 6-bit gadget from Theorem 10.20. The rank± (MF ) Ø 2 (n)
     and hence UPPcc (F ) Ø (n).

         In fact, as per the proof of Theorem 10.15, the above lower bound also
     holds for the inner product function, recovering Forster’s breakthrough
     result [59].

     UPPcc lower bounds for AC0 . The function ün ¶ g in Theorem 10.22
     is not in AC0 . It was open for several decades whether there is an AC0
     function F with UPPcc (F ) Ø n (1) . This question was originally posed
     by Babai, Frankl, and Simon [15] in a different but equivalent form;
     specifically, they asked for a function F solvable by PHcc protocols of
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     10.5. Sign-Rank Lower Bounds                                              151


     polylogarithmic cost but not by UPPcc protocols of polylogarithmic
     cost. Here, PHcc denotes the communication analog of the polynomial
     hierarchy.
          As described in Section 5.3, the question was resolved by Razborov
     and Sherstov [102]. They established the existence of a smooth dual
     witness for the fact that Minsky-Papert CNF ANDn1/3 ¶ ORn2/3 has
     threshold degree (n1/3 ) (see Theorem 5.6).
          We wish to cover an explicit construction of a smooth dual witness
     for an AC0 function. While such a construction is known for the Minsky-
     Papert DNF [121], we present one (based on techniques in [50]) that we
     feel is somewhat easier to understand. Unfortunately, our dual witness
        falls (very) slightly short of providing the necessary smoothness to
     apply Theorem 10.20. But we are able to obtain a dual witness ’ for
     the AC0 function ANDn1/3 ¶ ORn2/3 ¶ ülog2 n that is smooth enough to
     yield a UPPcc lower bound. This detailed proof sketch is particularly
     technical and may be skipped with no loss of continuity in this survey.
     Theorem 10.23. Let f = ANDn1/3 ¶ORn2/3 ¶ülog2 n , which is defined over
     N = n log2 n variables. Then deg± (f ) Ø D for some D = (n1/3 log n).
     Moreover, there is a dual polynomial ’ for this fact, such that |’(x)| Ø
     2≠D · 2≠N for al x œ {≠1, 1}N .

     deg± (ANDn1/3 ¶ORn2/3 ) Ø d for some d Ø (n1/3 / log n). Unfortunately,

     n≠2d · 2≠n for all x, whereas Theorem 10.20 requires | (x)| Ø 2≠d/2 2≠n .

     smooth as , but ’ witnesses a slightly larger degree lower bound of
     D = d log2 n. Thus, ’ is smooth enough relative to the degree bound it

     LTF ¶ MAJ circuit lower bounds.
     We saw in Section 10.4 that a PPcc lower bound for F implies F
     cannot be computed by small MAJ ¶ LTF circuits. Here, we show that a
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     156          Approximate Rank Lower Bounds from Approximate Degree


     UPPcc lower bound for F implies that F cannot be computed by small
     LTF ¶ MAJ circuits.

     Theorem 10.25. Suppose F (x, y) is computed by a LTF ¶ MAJ circuit
     C of size s + 1 Ø n. Then UPPcc (F ) Æ O(log(s)).
                                                         q
     Proof. Write the output of the circuit as sgn(w0 + si=1 wi · MAJi (x, y)),
     where MAJi (x, y) denotes the output of the ith MAJ gate in C (here,
     by a majority gate, we mean a gate that takes as input a subset of the
     variables of (x, y), each possibiy negated, and outputs ≠1 if the majority
     of those inputs equal ≠1). By perturbing weights if necessary, we may as-
                                                 q
     sume without loss of generality that w0 + si=1 wi MAJi (x, y) ”= 0 for any
     input pair (x, y). In the UPPcc protocol for F , Alice randomly chooses
     an i œ [s] with probability proportional to |wi | and sends
                                                              1 i to Bob, which
                                                                              2
     costs O(log s) bits. Alice and Bob then output sgn wi · MAJi (x, y) .
     Note that this costs O(log n) additional bits of communication. This is
     because it suffices for Alice to send to Bob the number of variables in x
     that are equal to ≠1 and connected by a wire to MAJi , as this enables
     Bob to determine the exact number of inputs to MAJi that are equal
     to ≠1.
         Similar to the proof of Fact 10.2, this protocol outputs F (x, y) with
     probability at least
                          3              q        4

         Combining Theorem 10.25 with Theorem 10.23 and Theorem 10.20

     Extending to LTF ¶ LTF circuits? Theorem 10.25 shows that every

     Open problems on threshold degree and sign-rank.
     It is open whether Theorem 10.20 holds without the smoothness condi-
     tion |Â(x)| Ø 2≠d/2 · 2≠n on the threshold degree dual witness Â. This
     may seem like a low-level technical question, but conceptually it is asking
     whether there is a generic query-to-communication-lifting theorem for
     UPP. That is, the threshold degree of a function f : {≠1, 1}n æ {≠1, 1}
     characterizes its UPP query complexity (Fact 10.2), while the sign-
     rank of a function F : {≠1, 1}n ◊ {≠1, 1}n æ {≠1, 1} characterizes its
     UPP communication complexity (Fact 10.6). If the requirement that
     |Â(x)| Ø 2≠d/2 · 2≠n could be removed from Theorem 10.20, it would
     generically translate UPP query lower bounds into UPP communica-
     tion lower bounds, rather than requiring “extra properties” of the dual
     object that was used to prove the query lower bound. It would also
     drastically simplify known sign-rank lower bounds (e.g., Theorem 10.23).

     Open Problem 10.26. Identify a “gadget” function g on a constant
     number of bits, such that for any function f : {≠1, 1}n æ {≠1, 1} with
     deg± (f ) Ø d, the following holds for the composed function F = f ¶ g:
     rank± (MF ) Ø 2d .

        A potentially easier open question is the following. It is known that
     UPP query complexity is not closed under intersection, i.e., there is a
     function h with threshold degree 1 such that H(x, y) = h(x) · h(y) has
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     160          Approximate Rank Lower Bounds from Approximate Degree


     threshold degree (n) [111], [112], [118]. It is open whether a similar
     result holds for UPP communication complexity. A generic lifting
     theorem for UPP would resolve this question, but so would giving a
     dual witness Â for the large threshold degree of H(x, y) such that Â
     has the requisite smoothness properties to invoke Theorem 10.20. The
     best-known result in this direction is that there exists a function F with
     UPP communication complexity O(log n), such that computing the
     AND of two copies of F evaluated over disjoint inputs requires UPP
     communication complexity (log2 n) [44].

     Open Problem 10.27. Is the class of problems with polylogarithmic
     UPP communication complexity closed under intersection?

     10.6   Extensions to Multiparty Communication Complexity

     A long line of work has shown that approximate degree lower bounds
     imply not only two-party randomized and quantum communication
     lower bounds as covered in this survey, but also state-of-the-art multi-
     party number-on-forehead lower bounds. These results have their own
     set of applications in circuit complexity and elsewhere. The interested
     reader is directed to [81, Section 8.3] for a survey on some early results
     in this line of work, as well as more recent papers [113], [116].
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107

                          Assorted Applications




     11.1   Secret Sharing Schemes

     Suppose a party, called the dealer, wishes to distribute a secret bit
     b œ {≠1, 1} amongst n parties, in the following sense. The deal will
     hand the ith party a bit xi , and it is required that all n parties together
     can reconstruct b by applying some known “reconstruction” function
     fn to their shares, while no “small” coalition of parties (say at most d
     parties) can guess b with any advantage over random guessing. This is
     called a secret-sharing scheme (for a 1-bit secret), and is a fundamental
     object of study in cryptography.
         As observed by [28], a dual polynomial Â for deg  Á (f ) Ø d precisely

     yields such a secret sharing scheme. Recall (Section 6) that Â can

     Â+1 = 2 max{Â(x), 0}. The dealer simply draws x ≥ Âb , and distributes
     xi to part i. To reconstruct the secret, the parties output f (x). The
     fact that Èf, ÂÍ > Á implies that if the secret bit b itself is chosen at
     random, then the probability of successful reconstruction is:

         Cryptographers are often interested in ensuring that the reconstruc-

     sharing scheme with perfect reconstruction, in which the reconstruction
     function is in AC0 , and no coalition of fewer than (n1≠” ) parties gains
     any advantage over random guessing in reconstructing the secret.
         Several works [27], [29], [30], [53] have studied variants of the above,
     such as allowing coalitions of size d to have a small but non-zero
     advantage over random guessing (which is related to Á-approximate
     weight (Section 10.4)), reconstruction by even simpler functions such as
     OR (which yields something called a visual secret sharing scheme), and
     the simplicity of sampling from the distribution Âb .

     11.2. Learning Algorithms                                                          163


     11.2    Learning Algorithms

     PAC Learning. Valiant’s Probably Approximately Correct (PAC) model
     [128] is intended to capture the task of supervised learning. In this model,
     there is some target function f : {≠1, 1}n æ {≠1, 1} that is unknown
     to the learning algorithm, but is assumed to come from some class
     of functions C. Here, C is referred to as a concept class. The learning
     algorithm is given access to labeled training data. This means the learner
     is fed some number m of labeled examples (x, f (x))—here, x is the
     example, and f (x) is the label. The number of labeled examples m
     consumed by the algorithm is referred to as the sample complexity of
     the learner.
         Each example x is assumed to be drawn independently from some
     fixed distribution D over {≠1, 1}n . We will be interested in distribution-
     independent PAC learning, which means that D is unknown to the
     learning algorithm and could be any distribution over {≠1, 1}n . The
     goal is for the learning algorithm to manage to predict f ’s labels on
     as-yet-unseen examples that are drawn from the same distribution as
     the training data. This means that the learner must output a hypothesis
     h, which is a function mapping {≠1, 1}n æ {≠1, 1}.1 The hypothesis h
     should be an Á-approximately correct predictor for f under distribution
     D. This means that Prx≥D [h(x) ”= f (x)] Æ Á for some desired accuracy
     parameter Á. The probability Prx≥D [h(x) ”= f (x)] is referred to as the
     generalization error of h, or sometimes as the loss of h (under the
     so-called zero-one loss function).2
         The learning algorithm is “probably approximately correct” with
     parameters Á and ”, if, with probability at least 1 ≠ ” over the random
     generation of labeled training data (and any internal randomness of the
     learning algorithm), the algorithm outputs an Á-approximately correct
     hypothesis.
         1
           For the learning algorithm to be considered computationally efficient, h should
     have a polynomial size representation, and it should be possible to efficiently evaluate
     h at any desired input x.
         2
           When learning real-valued rather than Boolean-valued functions, other loss
     functions make sense and often lead to more tractable learning problems. These
     include ¸2 -loss, ¸1 -loss, hinge loss, etc. In this survey, we only consider learning
     Boolean-valued functions under zero-one loss.
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     164                                                   Assorted Applications


         Prominent examples of concept classes studied in the learning theory
     literature include disjunctions and conjunctions, in which C consists
     of all functions of the form OR or AND applied to a subset of input
     variables or their negations, DNF formulas of size polynomial in n, and
     constant-depth circuits of size polynomial in n (i.e., AC0 ).
         As we will see shortly, another prominent concept class is the set of
     all halfspaces. This concept class is both interesting in its own right, and
     useful for “capturing” other concept classes, including some of those
     mentioned above.

     Agnostic learning. The PAC learning model described above makes
     the often-unrealistic assumption that the target function f resides in
     the concept class C. This is sometimes referred to as the “realizable
     case”, referring to the assumption there is some concept in C that “fully
     realizes” f by agreeing with f at all inputs x œ {≠1, 1}n .
         In many realistic scenarios, C is merely a good rather than perfect
     description of structure within f . In this situation, the optimal concept
     cú from C will itself fail to fit f perfectly, i.e., will have non-zero loss.
     This means that Prx≥D [cú (x) ”= f (x)] is small but not zero. Let us refer
     to this probability as opt. In the agnostic learning model [66], [72], the
     goal is to output a hypothesis h such that Prx≥D [h(x) ”= f (x)] Æ opt + Á.
     In words, the algorithm should output a hypothesis h that fits f nearly
     as well as the optimal concept from C.
         Another way to view the agnostic learning setting is to think of it as
     equivalent to the realizable setting, but where a small fraction (namely,
     opt) of adversarially training examples may be corrupted. In general,
     agnostic learning of any given concept class is a much more challenging
     task than (realizable) PAC learning, because the latter does not require
     learning in the presence of any noise in the training data, much less
     adversarially chosen classification noise.

     Bounding sample complexity via VC dimension. A common approach
     to learning algorithm design is for the algorithm to find a hypothesis h
     that “fits” the training data. This means that the training error, i.e.,
     the fraction of training inputs x such that h(x) ”= f (x), is small. The
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     11.2. Learning Algorithms                                               165


     algorithm that identifies a hypothesis h from a class of functions H that
     best fits the training data is typically referred to in machine learning as
     empirical risk minimization (ERM).
         There is, however, the risk of “overfitting” to the training data. This
     means the algorithm finds an h that fits the training data well, but h
     has poor generalization error. This means that, although h describes
     f well on the training data, h is not actually an accurate predicator
     under the “true” data distribution D. Intuitively, this can happen if
     h “hones in on” artifacts in the training data, i.e., properties of the
     specific sample used for training that are not representative of the true
     data distribution D.
         Suppose that the learning algorithm outputs a hypothesis h that
     is a member of some hypothesis class H. It turns out that overfitting
     to training data is unlikely to occur if H is a “simple” or “not-very-
     expressive” class of functions.
         One way of formalizing this is via so-called Vapnik-Chervonenkis
     dimension (VC-dimension). The definition of VC dimension is outside
     of the scope of this survey, but the salient point is the following. If a
     hypothesis class H has VC dimension at most D, then with sample
     complexity polynomial in D, log(1/”), and 1/Á, the generalization error
     and test error of all hypotheses differ by at most Á with probability at
     least 1 ≠ ” over the random choice of training data. This means that, if
     a learning algorithm outputs a hypothesis h that fits the training data
     well, then h will also have good generalization error.

     Halfspace learners. Recall that halfspaces (also called linear threshold
     functions, see Section 10.4) are functions of the form f (x1 , . . . , xn ) =
                q
     sgn(w0 + ni=1 wi xi ) for some weights w0 , . . . , wn œ R. It is known that
     the VC-dimension of halfspaces over n variables is n + 1.
         A set of labeled training examples is said to be linearly-separable if
     there is some halfspace f such that each training example x is assigned
     label f (x). There are many algorithms known that, given any set of
     linearly-separable examples, find a halfspace that correctly labels them.
     This can be done, for example, via any linear programming algorithm.
     But much simpler algorithms are known. These include the so-called
     Perceptron [104] and Winnow [85] algorithms.
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     166                                                   Assorted Applications


         Both Perceptron and Winnow find some weight vector that is con-
     sistent with the training data. In contrast, so-called support vector
     machines (SVM) find a particular weight vector, namely the one that
     maximizes the margin, which roughly corresponds to the closest to zero
                q
     that w0 + ni=1 wi xi gets across all training examples x (the notion of
     margin also arose in Section 10.5).
         Since the VC dimension of halfspaces is just n + 1, to PAC-learn
     the concept class of all halfspaces over n variables, it suffices to take a
     sample of size poly(n) and run any of the above algorithms to identify a
     halfspace consistent with the sample. In fact, there are halfspace-learning
     algorithms that are known that are robust to random classification
     noise in the training data. This refers to a variant of the PAC learning
     setting in which each training example has its label flipped with some
     small probability. Note that it is not known how to agnostically learn
     halfspaces in sub-exponential time—halfspace learning algorithms are
     not known to be robust to adversarial classification noise.

     Learning polynomial threshold functions. Given a labeled example
     x = (x1 , . . . , xn ) œ {≠1, 1}n , one can think of each xi as a (binary)
     feature. For example, if attempting to classify an email as spam or not
     spam, each coordinate of x may indicate certain properties of the email
     such as “does it contain the word ‘bank’?” or “does it contain spelling
     errors?” or “has the sender previously corresponded with the recipient?”
          Unfortunately, many natural functions f are not halfspaces—there
     is no weight vector w = (w0 , . . . , wn ) such that f (x) = sgn(w0 +
     qn
        i=1 wi xi ) for all x œ {≠1, 1} . In such situations, one can imagine
                                         n

     taking such a feature-vector x and expanding it into a larger vector, in
     which each coordinate contains a “derived feature” corresponding to
     a “combination” of the n “basic” features x1 , . . . , xn . Then even if f is
     not itself a halfspace, it may be one in the higher-dimensional space of
     “expanded feature vectors”.
          To make this concrete, recall that a degree-d polynomial threshold
     function f is the sign of a polynomial p : {≠1, 1}n æ R of total degree at
     most d. Any PTF f can be thought of as a halfspace over an expanded
                              !n"
     feature space of size Æd     , whereby each “expanded feature” is a product
     of at most d features in x.
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     11.2. Learning Algorithms                                                     167


         The above reframing of degree-d PTFs as halfspaces over expanded
     feature vectors immediately yields an algorithm that runs in time nO(d)
     for PAC learning degree-d PTFs. The algorithm samples a training set
     of size nO(d) , and for each example x in the training set, it expands
                       !n"
     x into the size- Æd    vector obtained by evaluating all monomials of
     degree at most d at x. One then applies a halfspace-learning algorithm
     to the resulting sample of expanded vectors. The total runtime of the
     algorithm—to expand each example in the sample and then apply the
     halfspace-learning algorithm to the expanded samples—is nO(d) .3

     Learning DNFs via PTFs. A famous challenge problem posed in
     Valiant’s seminal paper introducing the notion of PAC learning is to
     learn polynomial size DNF formulas in polynomial time. Unfortunately,
     we are very far from achieving this goal. The best-known algorithm
     currently runs in time exponential in n1/3 .
         It is easy to see via the Chebyshev-polynomial-based technique of
     Theorem 3.4 that the threshold degree of any DNF or CNF of size s is at
               Ô
     most O( n log s). This immediately yields an DNF learning algorithm
                                        Ô
     that runs in time exponential in n log s. Klivans and Servedio [75]
     improved the above degree bound from Õ(n1/2 ) to Õ(n1/3 ), and the
     resulting algorithm remains the fastest known today.

     Limitations on learning algorithms from threshold degree of sign-rank
     lower bounds. The fact that Minsky and Papert’s DNF has thresh-
     old degree (n1/3 ) (Theorem 5.6) means that Klivans and Servedio’s
     threshold degree upper bound for DNFs cannot be improved. One may
     wonder, though, whether one can improve on their algorithm by apply-
     ing a halfspace learning algorithm to a different set of derived features,
     one that does not consist of all monomials of degree at most d. Are
                                                           !n"
     there any “derived feature sets” of size smaller than Æd   over which all
     DNFs are halfspaces?
         It turns out that degree-at-most-d monomials are in fact the optimal
     feature set for learning DNFs via the above halfspace-based approach.
        3
          If the halfspace-learner used is a support-vector machine, the above algorithm
     roughly corresponds to running an SVM using the so-called polynomial kernel. See
     [71, Section 3.2] for additional discussion.
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     168                                                      Assorted Applications


     As we now explain, this follows from the exp( (n1/3 )) sign-rank lower
     bound of Razoborov and Sherstov [102] (Section 10.5)for the composition
     of the Minsky-Papert DNF with a constant-sized gadget.
         Specifically, Razborov and Sherstov construct a binary matrix M
     such that each row of M is the evaluation table of a DNF defined over
     n variables (more specifically, each row is the evaluation table of the
     Minsky-Papert DNF applied to a subset of input variables or their
                                                         1/3
     negations) and such that M has sign-rank r Ø 2 (n ) .
         Let C denote the class of polynomial size DNFs over n-bit inputs.
     Let M be the matrix whose rows are indexed by DNFs f œ C and whose
     columns are indexed by inputs x œ {≠1, 1}n , with Mf,x = f (x).
         Now suppose that F is a set of “feature functions” „ : {≠1, 1}n æ
     {≠1, 1} such that every f œ C can be expressed as a halfspace over the
     features. In other words, letting s + 1 = |F| and „0 , „1 , . . . , „s be an
     enumeration of the feature functions in F, with „0 (x) = 1 for all x,
     suppose that for each DNF f , there exist weights w0 , . . . , ws œ R such
     that                              A           B
                                          s
                                          ÿ
                            f (x) = sgn         wi „i (x) .                  (11.1)
                                          i=0
         This means that the sign-rank of M is at most s. To see this, let
     A be the |C| ◊ (s + 1) matrix with rows indexed by DNFs f in C and
     columns indexed by feature functions „i œ F, with Af,„i equal to the
     coefficient wi of „i in Equation (11.1). Let B be the (s + 1) ◊ 2n matrix,

     with B„i ,x = „i (x). Then Equation (11.1) implies that M equals the
     entry-wise sign of the product matrix A · B. This proves that the sign-
     rank of M is at most s + 1. Razborov and Sherstov’s result then implies
     that s Ø exp( (n1/3 )) as claimed.

     Agnostic Learning via Approximate Degree Upper Bounds. We have
     seen that if every function f in a concept class C has threshold degree at
     most d, then C can be PAC-learned in time nO(d) . However, threshold
     degree upper bounds are not known to yield learning algorithms in
     the more challenging agnostic setting. The fundamental challenge is
     that the empirical risk minimization problem is intractable when the
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     11.2. Learning Algorithms                                               169


     hypothesis class consists of all halfspaces. That is, while there are
     efficient procedures for finding a halfspace that perfectly fits the training
     data under the assumption that such a halfspace exists, without this
     assumption there is no analogous procedure to find the best-fitting
     halfspace (and in fact the problem is known to be NP-hard [64]).
          To obtain efficient agnostic learners, we turn to approximate degree
     upper bounds. Suppose that every c œ C is approximated to error Á/4
     by a polynomial of degree at most d. Then C can be agnostically learned
     in time nO(d) using the so-called ¸1 -polynomial regression algorithm of
     Kalai, Klivans, Mansour, and Servedio (KKMS) [71].
          Conceptually, the algorithm of KKMS solves a convex relaxation
     of the empirical risk minimization problem for degree-d polynomial
     threshold functions. Whereas finding the degree-d polynomial p that
     minimizes the zero-one loss of sgn(p(x)) is intractable, the algorithm
     of KKMS instead roughly finds the polynomial p minimizing the ¸1 -
     loss (see Equation (11.2) below). This minimization problem can be
     written as a linear program and hence can be solved in polynomial
     time. However, in order to guarantee that an optimal solution to the
     linear program actually yields an accurate hypothesis, we will need to
     require that every function c in the concept class C being learned have
     approximate degree at most d (instead of merely requiring threshold
     degree at most d as in the realizable case).
          In more detail, the algorithm first draws a sample of labeled training
     data of size nO(d) . Then, using linear programming, the algorithm finds
     the degree-d polynomial p that best fits the data under the ¸1 loss. This
     means that p minimizes
                                   ÿ
                                                      |p(x) ≠ f (x)|       (11.2)
                         (x,f (x)) in training data

     amongst all degree-d polynomials, where recall that m is the number of
     training examples consumed by the algorithm. Finally, the algorithm
     chooses a threshold t œ [≠1, 1] in a manner specified momentarily, and
     outputs the hypothesis h(x) = sgn (p(x) ≠ t). The threshold t is chosen
     to maximize the number of accurate predictions the algorithm makes
     on the training data, i.e., to minimize the number of training examples
     x such that h(x) ”= f (x).
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     170                                                                  Assorted Applications


         One may wish to think of p(x) as a kind of weighted prediction
     for f (x). If p(x) Ø 1 or p(x) Æ ≠1, then p is indicating, with high
     confidence, that f (x) = 1 or f (x) = ≠1 (though p(x) may make
     incorrect predictions on a small fraction of inputs x under distribution
     D, even when it indicates confidence therein). If p(x) œ (≠1, 1), then p
     is “indicating some uncertainty” regarding its prediction for f (x). The
     threshold t yields a “cutoff” to turn uncertain predictions into hard
     predictions.

     Sketch of the accuracy analysis for the algorithm. Following the
     analysis of [71], we first show that for the hypothesis h output by the
     algorithm, the number of training examples x such that h(x) ”= f (x)
     is at most 1/2 the ¸1 -error in Expression (11.2). To see this, observe
     that h(x) ”= f (x) only if the threshold t “separates” p(x) and f (x), i.e.,
     only if p(x) < t < f (x) or f (x) < t < p(x). If t were chosen uniformly
     at random from the interval [≠1, 1] (which has length 2) the probability
     that t splits p(x) and f (x) is at most |p(x) ≠ f (x)|/2. Hence, if t were
     chosen uniformly at random from [≠1, 1], the expected number of errors
     of h on the training set would be at most one half of Expression (11.2).
     This implies the existence of a threshold t achieving this training error.
     Since t is chosen to minimize the training error, the selected threshold
     does at least as well as this expectation.
          Second, we show that the fact that there exists a degree-d Á/4-
     approximating polynomial for every function in C implies that, in
     expectation over the random sample drawn from D, Expression (11.2) is
     at most m · (2opt + Á/4) for the polynomial p selected by the algorithm.
     To see this, let c œ C be the optimal classifier for the target function f ,
     i.e., c minimizes Prx≥S [c(x) ”= f (x)] amongst all concepts in C. Let pú
     be an Á/4-approximating polynomial for c. Then
                ÿ
                                   |pú (x) ≠ f (x)| Æ
      (x,f (x)) in training data
                                             ÿ
                                                                |pú (x) ≠ c(x)| + |c(x) ≠ f (x)|.
                                   (x,f (x)) in training data
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     11.2. Learning Algorithms                                            171


         Because pú is an Á/4-approximation for c, |pú (x) ≠ c(x)| Æ Á/4 for
     all x œ {≠1, 1}n . Meanwhile,
                       S                                    T
                    1            ÿ
                 EU                             |c(x) ≠ f (x)|V = 2opt,
                    m (x,f (x)) in training set

     where the expectation is over the randomly sampled training data.
     This follows by definition of opt and the fact that if c(x) = f (x)
     then |c(x) ≠ f (x)| = 0, while if c(x) ”= f (x), then |c(x) ≠ f (x)| = 2.
     So the expected value of Expression (11.2) with p = pú is at most
     m · (2opt + Á/4). Since the algorithm chooses the polynomial p that
     minimizes Expression (11.2) (and hence the p selected by the algorithm
     always does at least as well as pú in minimizing Expression (11.2)), the
     expected value of Equation (11.2) is in turn at most m · (2opt + Á/4)
     as claimed.
         Combined with the first step of the analysis, we conclude that the ex-
     pected number of errors that the algorithm’s chosen hypothesis h makes
     on the training set is at most m (opt + Á/8) (again, the expectation here
     is taken over the randomly sampled training data). Standard probabilis-
     tic analyses then imply that with noticeable probability, specifically at
     least Á/4, h achieves training error at most opt + Á/2, and in this event,
     VC theory implies that h achieves generalization error at most opt + Á
     as desired.
         One still needs to reduce the failure probability from 1 ≠ Á/4, to
     ”, where by failure we mean that the algorithm outputs an h with
     generalization error more than opt + Á. To achieve this, one can run
     the above algorithm O(log(1/”)/Á) times independently, to ensure that
     with high probability, at least one of the runs indeed outputs an h with
     the desired training error of at most opt + Á/2.

     11.3   Circuit Lower Bounds from Approximate Degree Upper
            Bounds

     Let C be a class of circuits, and imagine that for some function s(n) one
     has managed to prove that for every circuit C œ C of size at most s(n)
     over n-bit inputs, the function computed by C has threshold degree
     at most n ≠ 1 (just one below the maximum possible value, n). One
     can immediately conclude that no circuit in C of size s is capable of
     computing the parity function ün , as deg± (ün ) = n. For example,
     recall that a deep result established by Reichardt [103] states that any
     De Morgan formula of size s has approximate degree (and hence also
                            Ô
     threshold degree) O( s). This means that no De Morgan formula of
     size o(n2 ) can compute the parity function.
         If one shows that every C œ C of size at most s(n) has approximate de-
     gree o(n), then one can conclude that no C œ C can compute the majority
     function, since deg(MAJ)
                     Á          = (n). So, for example, by Reichardt’s result,
     no De Morgan formula of size o(n2 ) can compute the majority function.
         But many circuit classes of interest are powerful enough to compute
     parity and majority. For example, an important circuit class that we
     will consider shortly is AC0 ¶ MOD2 , which denotes the class of all AC0
     circuits augmented with a layer of parity gates above the inputs. Clearly,
     the parity function ün is computable with a size-1 circuit in AC0 ¶MOD2
     since ün itself is in MOD2 . Since parity has maximal threshold degree,
     it may seem that approximate degree and threshold degree are useless
     for proving lower bounds against such a circuit classes. But it turns out
     this is inaccurate.
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     11.3. Circuit Lower Bounds                                                  173

                                    OR    AND         OR   OR




                                    ⨁    ⨁            ⨁    ⨁
