      Figure 11.1: A depth-2 AC0 circuit with a layer of parity gates at the bottom.


         In Sections 11.3 and 11.3, we focus on implications for circuits
     computing the so-called inner-product-mod-2 function, IP2. The ideas
     in this section are due to Tal [125].

     Worst-case lower bounds from threshold degree upper bounds.
     For a given circuit class C and given class of functions G, let C ¶ G
     denote the circuit class in which circuits from C are augmented with
     a layer of leaf gates containing functions from G. So for example, if
     C is AC0 , and G is the set of all parity functions on 2n bits, i.e.,
     G = MOD2 := {‰S : S ™ [2n]}, then AC0 ¶ G denotes the circuit class
     AC0 ¶ MOD2 discussed above. See Figure 11.1 for an example.
          Henceforth, define G to be the class of all functions on 2n-bit inputs
     (x, y) with two-party deterministic communication complexity at most
     O(log n), and note that MOD2 is a subset of G, since the parity function
     has constant communication complexity. Let us assume that s(n) =
     poly(n) is a function such that every circuit C in C of size O(s(n))
     defined over s(n) inputs has threshold degree d Æ o(n/ log n). For
     example, if C is the class of De Morgan formulas, by Reichardt’s result
     we may take any s(n) = o((n/ log n)2 ).
          Then, as explained in the next paragraph, every circuit C Õ œ C ¶ G of
     size at most s(n) computes a function C Õ (x, y) with UPPcc complexity
     o(n). Since UPPcc (IP2) Ø (n) (see the remark after Corollary 10.22),
     it follows that no C œ C of size at most s(n) computes IP2.
          The UPPcc protocol for C Õ is analogous to the one in Fact 10.2,
     operating as follows. Since C Õ has size at most s(n), it has at most
     s(n) leaf gates, say, G1 , . . . , G¸ œ G for ¸ Æ s(n). Let us write C Õ =
     C(G1 (x, y), . . . , G¸ (x, y)), where the circuit C œ C itself has size at most
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     174                                                          Assorted Applications


     s(n). Let z œ {≠1, 1}¸ denote the vector (G1 (x, y), . . . , G¸ (x, y)). By
     assumption, there is some polynomial p(x) of degree d = o(n/ log n)
                                          q
     that sign-represents C, say, p(z) = S™[¸] p̂(S) · ‰S (z). Alice can use her

                                                                       !    "
     to |p̂(S)|. She can send S to Bob (this costs at most log2 ( s(n)   Æd ) Æ

     O(log n), this costs at most O(|S| · log n) = o(n) bits of communication.
     Bob can then output sgn(p̂(S)) · ‰S (z). Exactly as analyzed in Fact 10.2,
     this protocol outputs C(z) = C Õ (x) with probability strictly greater
     than 1/2.
         An immediate consequence of the above (along with Reichardt’s
     result [103]) is that, if C denotes the class of De Morgan formulas,
     circuits in C Õ = C ¶ G require size at least ((n/ log n)2 ) to compute
     IP2 [125]. This answered a question of Jukna [68], and it is essentially
     tight since IP2 is computed by quadratic size De Morgan formulas, even
     without any leaf gates from G.
         The next subsection shows that if C has sublinear approximate
     degree rather than just sublinear threshold degree, then circuits from
     C ¶ G cannot compute IP2 even on average.

     11.4          Parity is not in LTF ¶ AC0

     A famous result in circuit complexity is that parity, ün , is not in AC0
     [60], i.e., constant-depth circuits of unbounded fan-in that compute
     parity require exponential size. We cover one such proof of this result,
     due to [14], which is based on threshold degree. In fact, the analysis
     establishes the stronger result that parity is not in LTF ¶ AC0 , the class
     of polynomial size AC0 circuits with a threshold gate at the top (i.e.,
     the output gate computes a linear threshold function).
         The proof proceeds in two steps. First, show that any polynomial p
                   Ô
     of degree o( n) disagrees in sign with parity on at least a 1/2 ≠ o(1)
     fraction of inputs. That is, p(x) · ün (x) < 0 for at least (1/2 ≠ o(1)) · 2n
     inputs x in {≠1, 1}n .
         Second, show that for any LTF ¶ AC0 circuit C, there is a polynomial
     p of polylogarithmic degree that agrees in sign with C on 99% of inputs.6
     Together, these two results imply that parity cannot be computed by
           6
          More precisely, for any LTF ¶ AC0 circuit of size s and depth d, there is a
     polynomial p of degree (log s)O(d) · log(1/”) that agrees with C on a 1 ≠ ” fraction of
     inputs [65].
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     178                                                                    Assorted Applications


     any LTF ¶ AC0 circuit of1 polynomial
                                      2    size (in fact, LTF ¶ AC0 circuits
     require size at least exp n1/O(d) to compute parity).

     Step 1. Before proving the first step, let us first explain that the bound
     is tight: one can exactly compute parity on, say, 99% of all inputs by
                                   Ô
     “interpolating” the middle O( n) Hamming layers of the Boolean hyper-
     cube. That is, standard bounds on the Binomial coefficients imply that
     there is some constant c > 0 such that 99% of the 2n inputs in {≠1, 1}n
                                              Ô                 Ô
     have Hamming weight between n/2 ≠ c n and n/2 + c n. Define P (t)
                                                                             Ô
     via interpolation to be the unique polynomial of degree at most 2c n
                                                           Ô           Ô
     that evaluates to (≠1)t at all integers t œ [n/2 ≠ c n, n/2 + c n]. Let
     p(x) = P (|x|). Then p sign-represents (in fact, exactly computes) parity
     on 99% of all inputs.
         This construction turns out to be exactly optimal in terms of the
     number of inputs at which it agrees in sign with parity. It can be checked
                                                                            !n"
     that p is a degree-d polynomial that disagrees in sign with parity on Æk
                                            Ô           !   "
     inputs where k = (n ≠ d)/2.7 If d = o( n), then Æk   n
                                                              Ø (1/2 ≠ o(1)) · 2n .
         To prove that this is optimal, it suffices to show that for any set
                                    !n"
     S ™ {≠1, 1}n of size at most Æk    , any degree d = n ≠ 2k polynomial
     p must disagree in sign with parity on at least one input outside of
     S. This turns out to be equivalent to constructing a dual polynomial
     Â : {≠1, 1}n æ R of pure high degree at least d = n ≠ 2k such that:

       (a) Â(x) = 0 for all x œ S, i.e., Â vanishes on S.

       (b) Â has perfect correlation with parity, i.e., Â(x) · ün (x) Ø 0 for all
           x œ {≠1, 1}n .

     Intuitively, such a Â is a dual witness to the high threshold degree of ün
     that “ignores” inputs outside of S. Hence, Â witnesses that any PTF p
     for parity must have degree at least d, regardless of how p behaves at
     inputs in S.

     11.4. Parity is not in LTF ¶ AC0                                             179


     polynomial q of degree at most k that vanishes on S. That is, since
              !n"
     there are Æk   coefficients of q, we can choose them so as to ensure that
     q(x) = 0 for all x œ S. The polynomial q 2 then has degree at most 2k,
     and q(x) Ø 0 for all x œ {≠1, 1}n . The function Â(x) := ün (x) · q 2 (x) is
     a non-zero dual polynomial with pure high degree n ≠ 2k (see the proof
     of Theorem 6.4) that is perfectly correlated with parity, as required.

     Step 2. The key is to show that for any AC0 circuit C of depth d
     and size s, there is a polynomial p of degree (log s/”)O(d) that exactly
     computes C on a 1 ≠ ” fraction of inputs. One then obtains a polynomial
     that sign-represents any LTF ¶ AC0 circuit of size by s on 99% of inputs
     by, first, exactly computing each constituent AC0 circuit on all but
     a 1/(100s) fraction of inputs—which requires degree just (log s)O(d) —
     and then taking the appropriate linear combination of the resulting
     polynomials, i.e., with the coefficients of the linear combination given
     by the weights of the LTF gate.

     Probabilistic Degree. The technical core of the key result is an upper
     bound of O(log(n) · log(1/”)) on the so-called ”-error probabilistic degree
     of OR. Here, a ”-error probabilistic polynomial of degree d over the reals
     for a circuit C(x1 , . . . , xn ) is a random polynomial P (x1 , . . . , xn ) such
     that for any x œ {≠1, 1}n , PrP [C(x) ”= P (x)] Æ ”. That is, P is drawn
     at random from some distribution over degree-d polynomials, and the
     requirement is that for every fixed input x, P (x) is very likely to exactly
     equal C(x). Before proving the upper bound of O(log(n) · log(1/”)) on
     the probabilistic degree of OR, we explain why it implies an upper
     bound of O(logO(d) (n)) on the probabilistic degree of any AC0 circuit
     of depth d.

     From Probabilistic Polynomials for OR and AND to AC0 . Let C be
     an AC0 circuit of size s. Each AND and OR gate of C has a 1/(100s)-
     error probabilistic polynomial of degree O(log2 (s)). For each gate, draw
     such a polynomial at random and consider the gate-by-gate composition
     of the resulting polynomials. This composed polynomial p has degree
     log(s)O(d) . For each input x, by a union bound over all s gates of C,
The version of record is available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1561/0400000107


     180                                                                Assorted Applications


     the probability that p(x) ”= C(x) is at most s · (1/(100s)) Æ 1/100.
     Hence, the expected number of inputs x at which p(x) = C(x) is at least
     .99 · 2n . By averaging, there exists some polynomial p of degree at most
     log(s)O(d) that agrees with C on at least 99% of all inputs.

     Probabilistic Polynomial for OR. The ”-error probabilistic polynomial
     for OR that we cover is due to [19]. The construction is reminiscent of
     an earlier one over finite fields due to Razborov [100].
         We begin with a construction that achieves ” = 1/(2e). Let F be a
     random collection of 1 + log n subsets of [n] generated as follows. For
     i = 0, 1, . . . , log n, the i’th subset, Si , of F is generated by independently
     including each j œ [n] in Si with probability 2≠i . Then define
                                                         Q                      R
                                                   Ÿ            ÿ
                   P (x1 , . . . , xn ) = ≠1 + 2         a1 +         (xj ≠ 1)/2b .
                                                   SœF          jœS

         Observe that P has degree |F| = 1 + log n. Showing that this indeed
     yields a (1/(2e))-error probabilistic polynomial for OR relies on the
     following two observations. First, if x œ OR≠1 (1), so that x = 1n , then
     P (x) = 1 with probability 1 over the choice of P . This holds because
     (xj ≠ 1)/2 will equal 0 for every j. Hence, P always has the desired
     behavior on x = 1n .
         Second, so long as there is at least one set S œ F such that there
     is exactly one j œ S with xj = ≠1, then P (x) = ≠1. We now show
     that this occurs with probability at least 1/(2e) over the random choice
     of subsets in F. This guarantees that the resulting distribution over
     polynomials P has the desired behavior on any x œ OR≠1 (≠1).
         Specifically, fix an x œ {≠1, 1}n with |x| Ø 1, and let T denote the
     set of coordinates of x that are equal to ≠1. Let 2i be the smallest
     power of 2 greater than or equal to |x|. The probability that exactly
     one coordinate j from T is in Si is

     ÿ
           Pr[j œ Si ] · Pr[no other elements of T are in Si ] = |x| · 2≠i · (1 ≠ 2≠i )|x|≠1 .

     11.4. Parity is not in LTF ¶ AC0                                       181


        This probability is at least 1/(2e). To see this, observe that |x|·2≠i >
     1/2, while
                    (1 ≠ 2≠i )|x|≠1 Ø (1 ≠ 2≠|x| )|x|≠1 Ø 1/e,
     where the final inequality invokes Fact 2.2.
         To drive the error down from 1/(2e) to ”, one generates ¸ =
     O(log(1/”)) independent “copies” of each set Si and adds all of the
     copies to F. That is, for each copy of Si , each coordinate j œ [n] is
     included independently with probability 2≠i . The probability that none
     of the copies of Si contain exactly one element of T is at most 1/(2e)¸ .
     Hence, for a suitable ¸ = O(log(1/”)), this probability is at most ”.
     Hence, P is a ”-error probabilistic approximation for OR of degree at
     most O(log(n) · log(1/”)).
